\widetilde{t}:=\mathrm{diag}(1,\dots,1,-1,-1)\in SL_{n}(q)
. Then we have

C_{SL_{n}(q)}(\widetilde{t})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in GL_{n-2}(q),B\in GL_{2}(q),\mathrm{det}(A)\mathrm{det}(B)=1\right\}.

It is easy to note that

\widetilde{T}:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in W,B\in V,\mathrm{det}(A)\mathrm{det}(B)=1\right\}

is a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
C_{SL_{n}(q)}(\widetilde{t})
. Let
T
denote the image of
\widetilde{T}
in
PSL_{n}(q)
. As the centralizer of
t
in
PSL_{n}(q)
is the image of
C_{SL_{n}(q)}(\widetilde{t})
in
PSL_{n}(q)
, we have that
T
is a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t)
. We assume
S
to be a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
PSL_{n}(q)
containing
T
. Since
C_{S}(t)=T\in\mathrm{Syl_{2}}(C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t))
, we have that
\langle t\rangle
is fully
\mathcal{F}_{S}(PSL_{n}(q))
-centralized.

Let
K_{1}
be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&I_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in SL_{n-2}(q)\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
, and let
K_{2}
be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}I_{n-2}&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}\ :\ B\in SL_{2}(q)\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
. Clearly,
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are normal subgroups of
C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t)
isomorphic to
SL_{n-2}(q)
and
SL_{2}(q)
, respectively. Define
X_{1}
to be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&I_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in W\cap SL_{n-2}(q)\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
, and define
X_{2}
to be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}I_{n-2}&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}\ :\ B\in V\cap SL_{2}(q)\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
.

Note that
X_{1}=T\cap K_{1}\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(K_{1})
and
X_{2}=T\cap K_{2}\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(K_{2})
. Define

\mathcal{C}_{i}:=\mathcal{F}_{X_{i}}(K_{i})

for
i\in\{1,2\}
. By
[
11
, Part I, Proposition 6.2]
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are normal subsystems of
\mathcal{F}_{T}(C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t))
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{S}(PSL_{n}(q))
.
If
q\equiv 1
or
7\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
, then the components of
C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)
are precisely the subsystems
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
. If
q\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
, then
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is the only component of
C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)
.

Proof.

Set
C:=C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t)
. It is easy to note that the
2
-components of
C
are precisely the quasisimple elements of
\{K_{1},K_{2}\}
. As
n\geq 6
and as
K_{1}\cong SL_{n-2}(q)
and
K_{2}\cong SL_{2}(q)
, it follows that the
2
-components of
C
are
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
if
q\neq 3
, and that
K_{1}
is the only
2
-component of
C
if
q=3
.

By Lemma
3.21
,
K_{1}/Z(K_{1})
is not a Goldschmidt group. If
q\neq 3
, then the lemma just cited also shows that
K_{2}/Z(K_{2})
is a Goldschmidt group if and only if
q\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
.

Now we apply Proposition
2.16
to conclude that
\mathcal{F}_{T\cap K_{1}}(K_{1})
and
\mathcal{F}_{T\cap K_{2}}(K_{2})
are precisely the components of
\mathcal{F}_{T}(C)
if
q\equiv 1
or
7\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
, and that
\mathcal{F}_{T\cap K_{1}}(K_{1})
is the only component of
\mathcal{F}_{T}(C)
if
q\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
. This completes the proof because
C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)=\mathcal{F}_{T}(C)
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{F}_{T\cap K_{1}}(K_{1})
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}=\mathcal{F}_{T\cap K_{2}}(K_{2})
.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{S}(PSL_{n}(q))
.
Then the factor system
C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)/X_{1}X_{2}
is nilpotent.

Proof.

Set
C:=C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t)
. It is easy to note that
X_{1}X_{2}=K_{1}K_{2}\cap T
. By Lemma
2.11
,
C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)/X_{1}X_{2}
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
C/K_{1}K_{2}
. The factor group
C/K_{1}K_{2}
is abelian. This easily implies that
C/K_{1}K_{2}
has a nilpotent
2
-fusion system. Hence
C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)/X_{1}X_{2}
is nilpotent.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
A\in W
and
B\in V
such that
\mathrm{det}(A)\mathrm{det}(B)=1
. Let

m:=\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}Z(SL_{n}(q))\in T.

Then we have
m\in Z(\mathcal{C}_{1}\langle m\rangle)
if and only if
A\in Z(GL_{n-2}(q))
.

Proof.

By
[
34
, Proposition 1]
, we have
\mathcal{C}_{1}\langle m\rangle=\mathcal{F}_{X_{1}\langle m\rangle}(K_{1}\langle m\rangle)
.

If
A\in Z(GL_{n-2}(q))
, then
m
is central in
K_{1}\langle m\rangle
, which implies that
m
lies in the center of
\mathcal{C}_{1}\langle m\rangle
.

We show now that if
A\not\in Z(GL_{n-2}(q))
, then
m\not\in Z(\mathcal{C}_{1}\langle m\rangle)
. Assume to the contrary that
A\not\in Z(GL_{n-2}(q))
, but
m\in Z(\mathcal{C}_{1}\langle m\rangle)
. Clearly,
m\in Z(X_{1}\langle m\rangle)
. So
m
centralizes
X_{1}
. It easily follows that
A
centralizes
W\cap SL_{n-2}(q)
. Using Sylow’s theorem, we may see from Lemma
3.17
that any element
A_{0}
of
W
which centralizes
W\cap SL_{n-2}(q)
without being central in
GL_{n-2}(q)
is
SL_{n-2}(q)
-conjugate to an element of
W
different from
A_{0}
. As
A
centralizes
W\cap SL_{n-2}(q)
, but
A\not\in Z(GL_{n-2}(q))
, it follows that
A
is
SL_{n-2}(q)
-conjugate to an element
A^{\prime}\in W
with
A\neq A^{\prime}
. As
\mathrm{det}(A)=\mathrm{det}(A^{\prime})
, we have
A^{\prime}=A^{\prime\prime}A
for some
A^{\prime\prime}\in W\cap SL_{n-2}(q)
. Now, it follows that
m
is
K_{1}
-conjugate to

\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}Z(SL_{n}(q))=\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime\prime}&\\
&I_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}Z(SL_{n}(q))\in X_{1}\langle m\rangle.

So
m
is
K_{1}
-conjugate to an element of
X_{1}\langle m\rangle
which is different from
m
. Therefore,
m\not\in Z(\mathcal{C}_{1}\langle m\rangle)
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Set
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{S}(PSL_{n}(q))
and
\mathcal{G}:=C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)
. Then
\mathfrak{hnp}(C_{\mathcal{G}}(X_{1}))=X_{2}
.

Proof.

Set
C:=C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t)
. Note that
C^{\prime}=K_{1}K_{2}
.

By
[
24
, Chapter 7, Theorem 3.4]
, we have
\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\mathcal{G}}(X_{1}))=C_{T}(X_{1})\cap C_{C}(X_{1})^{\prime}\leq C_{T}(X_{1})\cap C^{\prime}=C_{T}(X_{1})\cap X_{1}X_{2}=Z(X_{1})X_{2}
. As
\mathfrak{hnp}(C_{\mathcal{G}}(X_{1}))\leq\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\mathcal{G}}(X_{1}))
, it follows that
\mathfrak{hnp}(C_{\mathcal{G}}(X_{1}))\leq Z(X_{1})X_{2}
.

Let
P
be a subgroup of
C_{T}(X_{1})
and let
\varphi
be a
2^{\prime}
-element of
\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{C}(X_{1})}(P)
. By
[
37
, 8.2.7]
, we have

[P,\langle\varphi\rangle]=[P,\langle\varphi\rangle,\langle\varphi\rangle]\leq[\mathfrak{hnp}(C_{\mathcal{G}}(X_{1}))\cap P,\langle\varphi\rangle]\leq[Z(X_{1})X_{2}\cap P,\langle\varphi\rangle].

Since
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{C}(X_{1})}(P)
,
K_{2}\trianglelefteq C
and
X_{2}=T\cap K_{2}
, it follows
[P,\langle\varphi\rangle]\leq X_{2}
. Consequently,
\mathfrak{hnp}(C_{\mathcal{G}}(X_{1}))\leq X_{2}
.

On the other hand, since
K_{2}\leq O^{2}(C_{C}(X_{1}))
, we have
X_{2}\leq\mathfrak{hnp}(C_{\mathcal{G}}(X_{1}))
by
[
19
, Theorem 1.33]
.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Set
C:=C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t)
. Then
\mathrm{Aut}_{C}(X_{1})
is a
2
-group.

Proof.

Let
m\in N_{C}(X_{1})
. We have

m=\begin{pmatrix}M_{1}&\\
&M_{2}\end{pmatrix}Z(SL_{n}(q))

for some
M_{1}\in GL_{n-2}(q)
and some
M_{2}\in GL_{2}(q)
with
\mathrm{det}(M_{1})\mathrm{det}(M_{2})=1
. Let
A\in W\cap SL_{n-2}(q)
and

x:=\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&I_{2}\end{pmatrix}Z(SL_{n}(q))\in X_{1}.

As
m
normalizes
X_{1}
, we have

\begin{pmatrix}A^{M_{1}}&\\
&I_{2}\end{pmatrix}Z(SL_{n}(q))=x^{m}\in X_{1}.

This easily implies that
A^{M_{1}}\in W\cap SL_{n-2}(q)
. It follows that
M_{1}
normalizes
W\cap SL_{n-2}(q)
. By
[
36
, Theorem 1]
, we have
N_{GL_{n-2}(q)}(W\cap SL_{n-2}(q))=WC_{GL_{n-2}(q)}(W)
. It follows that
c_{m}|_{X_{1},X_{1}}
is a
2
-automorphism.
∎

Define
T_{1}
to be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&I_{n-2}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in V\cap SL_{2}(q)\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
and
T_{2}
to be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}I_{2}&&\\
&B&\\
&&I_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ B\in V_{2}\cap SL_{n-4}(q)\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
. Clearly,
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are subgroups of
X_{1}
. Recall that we use
u
to denote
t_{\{1,2\}}\in X_{1}
. The following lemma sheds light on some properties of the centralizer fusion system
C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle)
.

Lemma 5.8
.

The following hold.

(i)

We have
C_{X_{1}}(u)\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(C_{K_{1}}(u))
. In particular,
\langle u\rangle
is fully
\mathcal{C}_{1}
-centralized.

(ii)

\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle))=T_{1}T_{2}
.

(iii)

If
n=6
and
q\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
, then
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are the only subgroups of
\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle))
which are isomorphic to
Q_{8}
and strongly closed in
C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle)
.

(iv)

If
n\geq 7
and
q\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
, then
T_{1}
is the only subgroup of the intersection
\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle))\cap C_{X_{1}}(T_{2})
which is isomorphic to
Q_{8}
and strongly closed in
C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle)
.

(v)

Let
C_{1}
be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&I_{n-2}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in SL_{2}(q)\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
and
C_{2}
be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}I_{2}&&\\
&B&\\
&&I_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in SL_{n-4}(q)\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
. Then any component of
C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle)
lies in
\{\mathcal{F}_{T_{1}}(C_{1}),\mathcal{F}_{T_{2}}(C_{2})\}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{F}_{T_{1}}(C_{1})
is a component if and only if
q\equiv 1
or
7\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
, and
\mathcal{F}_{T_{2}}(C_{2})
is a component if and only if
n\geq 7
or
q\equiv 1
or
7\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
.

Proof.

Clearly,
C_{K_{1}}(u)
is the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&&\\
&B&\\
&&I_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in GL_{2}(q),B\in GL_{n-4}(q),\mathrm{det}(A)\mathrm{det}(B)=1\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
. Let
\widetilde{W}
be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&&\\
&B&\\
&&I_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in V,B\in V_{2},\mathrm{det}(A)\mathrm{det}(B)=1\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
. Clearly, we have
\widetilde{W}\leq C_{X_{1}}(u)
. It is easy to note that
\widetilde{W}
is a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
C_{K_{1}}(u)
. Thus
C_{X_{1}}(u)=\widetilde{W}\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(C_{K_{1}}(u))
. Hence (i) holds.

We have
C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle)=\mathcal{F}_{C_{X_{1}}(u)}(C_{K_{1}}(u))=\mathcal{F}_{\widetilde{W}}(C_{K_{1}}(u))
. The focal subgroup theorem
[
24
, Chapter 7, Theorem 3.4]
implies that
\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle))=\widetilde{W}\cap(C_{K_{1}}(u))^{\prime}
. It is easy to see that
(C_{K_{1}}(u))^{\prime}=C_{1}C_{2}
, where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are as in (v). We thus have
\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle))=T_{1}T_{2}
. Hence (ii) holds.

Now we turn to the proofs of (iii) and (iv). Assume that
q\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
. Clearly,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are normal subgroups of
C_{K_{1}}(u)
and we have
T_{1}=C_{1}\cap\widetilde{W}
,
T_{2}=C_{2}\cap\widetilde{W}
. This implies that
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are strongly closed in
C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle)
. By Lemma
3.12
, we have
T_{1}\cong Q_{8}
and, if
n=6
, we also have
T_{2}\cong Q_{8}
. Clearly, any strongly
C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle)
-closed subgroup of
\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\langle u\rangle))=T_{1}T_{2}
is strongly closed in
\mathcal{F}_{T_{1}T_{2}}(C_{1}C_{2})
. Hence, in order to prove (iii), it suffices to show that if
n=6
, then
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are the only strongly
\mathcal{F}_{T_{1}T_{2}}(C_{1}C_{2})
-closed subgroups of
T_{1}T_{2}
which are isomorphic to
Q_{8}
. Similarly, in order to prove (iv), it suffices to show that if
n\geq 7
, then
T_{1}
is the only subgroup of
T_{1}T_{2}
which centralizes
T_{2}
, which is isomorphic to
Q_{8}
, and which is strongly closed in
\mathcal{F}_{T_{1}T_{2}}(C_{1}C_{2})
.

Continue to assume that
q\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
. In order to prove the two statements just mentioned, we need some observations. As
C_{1}\cong SL_{2}(q)
, we have that
C_{1}
is not
2
-nilpotent. So
\mathcal{F}_{T_{1}}(C_{1})
is not nilpotent by
[
39
, Theorem 1.4]
. Again by
[
39
, Theorem 1.4]
, it follows that
\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{1}}(T_{1})
is not a
2
-group. So
\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{1}}(T_{1})
has an element of order
3
. Similarly, if
n=6
, then
\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{2}}(T_{2})
has an element of order
3
. It follows that there is an element
\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{1}C_{2}}(T_{1}T_{2})
such that
\alpha|_{T_{1},T_{1}}
has order
3
, while
\alpha|_{T_{2},T_{2}}=\mathrm{id}_{T_{2}}
. Moreover, if
n=6
, then there is an element
\beta\in\mathrm{Aut}_{C_{1}C_{2}}(T_{1}T_{2})
such that
\beta|_{T_{1},T_{1}}=\mathrm{id}_{T_{1}}
, while
\beta|_{T_{2},T_{2}}
has order
3
.

Continue to assume that
q\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
. If
n=6
, then the observations in the preceding two paragraphs show together with Lemma
2.14
that
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are the only strongly
\mathcal{F}_{T_{1}T_{2}}(C_{1}C_{2})
-closed subgroups of
T_{1}T_{2}
which are isomorphic to
Q_{8}
. As observed above, this is enough to conclude that (iii) holds. If
n\geq 7
, then we may apply the observations in the preceding two paragraphs together with Lemma
2.14
to conclude that if
T_{0}
is a strongly
\mathcal{F}_{T_{1}T_{2}}(C_{1}C_{2})
-closed subgroup of
T_{1}T_{2}
such that
T_{0}\cong Q_{8}
and such that
T_{0}
centralizes
T_{2}
, then
T_{0}=T_{1}
. As observed above, this is enough to conclude that (iv) holds.

It remains to prove (v). It is easy to note that the
2
-components of
C_{K_{1}}(u)
are precisely the quasisimple elements of
\{C_{1},C_{2}\}
. So (v) can be obtained from Proposition
2.16
and Lemma
3.21
.
∎

Let
G
be as in Hypothesis
5.1
. The group
G
realizes the
2
-fusion system of
PSL_{n}(q)
. So, if
R
is a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
G
, then
\mathcal{F}_{S}(PSL_{n}(q))\cong\mathcal{F}_{R}(G)
. For the sake of simplicity, we will identify
S
with a Sylow
2
-subgroup
R
of
G
and
\mathcal{F}_{S}(PSL_{n}(q))
with
\mathcal{F}_{R}(G)
. Hence we will work under the following hypothesis.

Hypothesis 5.9
.

We will treat
G
as a group with
S\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(G)
and
\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)=\mathcal{F}_{S}(PSL_{n}(q))
.

The following lemma will play a key role in the proof of Theorem
5.2
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
x
be an involution of
S
such that
C_{S}(x)\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(C_{G}(x))
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a component of
\mathcal{F}_{C_{S}(x)}(C_{G}(x))
, and let
k
be a natural number with
3\leq k<n
. Then the following hold.

(i)

There is a unique
2
-component
Y
of
C_{G}(x)
such that
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{F}_{C_{S}(x)\cap Y}(Y)
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{C}
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{k}(q)
, then we either have that
Y/O(Y)\cong SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
for some nontrivial odd prime power
q^{*}
and some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
with
q\sim\varepsilon q^{*}
; or
k=3
,
(q+1)_{2}=4
, and
Y/Z^{*}(Y)\cong M_{11}
.

(iii)

If
\mathcal{C}
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of a nontrivial quotient of
SL_{k}(q^{2})
, then
Y/O(Y)
is isomorphic to a nontrivial quotient of
SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
for some nontrivial odd prime power
q^{*}
and some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
with
q^{2}\sim\varepsilon q^{*}
.

In order to prove Lemma
5.10
, we need the following observation.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
k\geq 6
be a natural number satisfying
P(k)
. If
q_{0}
is a nontrivial odd prime power and
H
is a known finite simple group realizing the
2
-fusion system of
PSL_{k}(q_{0})
, then
H\cong PSL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
for some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
and some nontrivial odd prime power
q^{*}
with
\varepsilon q^{*}\sim q_{0}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that any known finite simple group
H
satisfies (

⁢
C
K

). Without using the CFSG, this is a priori not clear. It can be deduced from
[
29
, Proposition 5.2.9]
if
H
is alternating, from
[
29
, Table 4.5.1]
if
H
is a finite simple group of Lie type in odd characteristic, and from
[
29
, Table 5.3]
if
H
is sporadic. If
H
is a finite simple group of Lie type in characteristic
2
, then
H
satisfies (

⁢
C
K

) since, in this case, no involution centralizer in
H
has a
2
-component (see
[
5
, 47.8 (3)]
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.10
.

Since
G
satisfies (

⁢
C
K

), we have that
Y/Z^{*}(Y)
is a known finite simple group for each
2
-component
Y
of
C_{G}(x)
. Proposition
2.16
implies that there is a unique
2
-component
Y
of
C_{G}(x)
with
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{F}_{C_{S}(x)\cap Y}(Y)
. Thus (i) holds.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{k}(q_{0})/Z
, where either
q_{0}=q
and
Z=1
, or
q_{0}=q^{2}
and
Z\leq Z(SL_{k}(q^{2}))
. In order to prove (ii) and (iii), we need the following three claims.

(1)
The
2
-fusion systems of
Y/Z^{*}(Y)
and
PSL_{k}(q_{0})
are isomorphic.

As
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{F}_{C_{S}(x)\cap Y}(Y)
, we have that the
2
-fusion system of
Y
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{k}(q_{0})/Z
. So, by Corollary
2.12
, the
2
-fusion system of
Y/O(Y)
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{k}(q_{0})/Z
. Lemma
2.13
implies that the
2
-fusion systems of
Y/Z^{*}(Y)
and
PSL_{k}(q_{0})
are isomorphic.

(2)
We have
Y/Z^{*}(Y)\cong PSL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
for some nontrivial odd prime power
q^{*}
and some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
with
q_{0}\sim\varepsilon q^{*}
; or
k=3
,
(q_{0}+1)_{2}=4
and
Y/Z^{*}(Y)\cong M_{11}
.

If
k=3
, then it follows from (1) and Proposition
4.2
. If
k\in\{4,5\}
, then it follows from (1) together with Propositions
4.5
and
4.6
. Assume now that
k\geq 6
. By Hypothesis
5.1
and since
k<n
, we have that
k
satisfies
P(k)
. Since
Y/Z^{*}(Y)
is a known finite simple group, the claim follows from (1) and Lemma
5.11
.

(3)
Suppose that
Y/Z^{*}(Y)\cong PSL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
, where
q^{*}
and
\varepsilon
are as in (2). Then we have
Y/O(Y)\cong SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/U
, where
U\leq Z(SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
and the index of
U
in
Z(SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
is equal to the
2
-part of
|Z(SL_{k}(q_{0}))/Z|
.

The group
Y/O(Y)
is a perfect central extension of
PSL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
. Since
Y/O(Y)
is core-free, the center of
Y/O(Y)
is a
2
-group. So, by Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
, there is a central subgroup
U
of
SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
with
Y/O(Y)\cong SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/U
. The claim now follows from

\displaystyle|PSL_{k}(q_{0})|_{2}|Z(SL_{k}(q_{0}))/Z|_{2}

\displaystyle=|SL_{k}(q_{0})/Z|_{2}

\displaystyle=|Y|_{2}

\displaystyle=|Y/Z^{*}(Y)|_{2}|Z(Y/O(Y))|

\displaystyle=|PSL_{k}(q_{0})|_{2}|Z(SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))/U|.

Here, the second equality follows from the fact that
Y
realizes
\mathcal{C}
, the third one holds since
|Z^{*}(Y)|_{2}=|Z^{*}(Y)/O(Y)|_{2}=|Z(Y/O(Y))|_{2}=|Z(Y/O(Y))|
, and the fourth one follows from (1).

Assume that
q_{0}=q
and
Z=1
. By (2) and (3), one of the following hold: either
k=3
,
(q+1)_{2}=4
and
Y/Z^{*}(Y)\cong M_{11}
; or
Y/O(Y)\cong SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/U
, where
q^{*}
is a nontrivial odd prime power,
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
,
q\sim\varepsilon q^{*}
,
U\leq Z(SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
and the index of
U
in
Z(SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
is equal to the
2
-part of
|Z(SL_{k}(q))|
. Assume that the latter holds. As
q\sim\varepsilon q^{*}
, we have
(q-1)_{2}=(\varepsilon q^{*}-1)_{2}=(q^{*}-\varepsilon)_{2}
. Since
|Z(SL_{k}(q))|=(k,q-1)
and
|Z(SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))|=(k,q^{*}-\varepsilon)
, it follows that the
2
-part of
|Z(SL_{k}(q))|
is equal to the
2
-part of
|Z(SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))|
. It follows that
U=O(Z(SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})))=O(SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
. This completes the proof of (ii).

Assume now that
q_{0}=q^{2}
. Then, since
q^{2}\equiv 1\ \mathrm{mod}\ 4
, (2) und (3) imply that
Y/O(Y)
is isomorphic to a nontrivial quotient of
SL_{k}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
for some nontrivial odd prime power
q^{*}
and some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
with
q^{2}\sim\varepsilon q^{*}
. Thus (iii) holds.
∎

6.
2
-components of involution centralizers

In this section, we continue to assume Hypotheses
5.1
and
5.9
. We will use the notation introduced in the last section without further explanation.

The main goal of this section is to describe the
2
-components and the solvable
2
-components of the centralizers of involutions of
G
.

6.1.
The subgroups
K
and
L
of
C_{G}(t)

We start by considering
C_{G}(t)
. Let
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)=\mathcal{F}_{S}(PSL_{n}(q))
. Since
\langle t\rangle
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-centralized, we have that
T=C_{S}(t)\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(C_{G}(t))
. Also, note that
\mathcal{F}_{T}(C_{G}(t))=C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)=\mathcal{F}_{T}(C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t))
.

Proposition 6.1
.

There is a unique
2
-component
K
of
C_{G}(t)
such that
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{F}_{T\cap K}(K)
. We have
K/O(K)\cong SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
for some nontrivial odd prime power
q^{*}
and some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
with
q\sim\varepsilon q^{*}
. Moreover,
K
is a normal subgroup of
C_{G}(t)
.

Proof.

Set
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)
. By Lemma
5.3
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is a component of
C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)
. Lemma
5.10
(i) implies that there is a unique
2
-component
K
of
C_{G}(t)
such that
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{F}_{T\cap K}(K)
. By definition, the component
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{n-2}(q)
. Lemma
5.10
(ii) implies that
K/O(K)\cong SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
for some nontrivial odd prime power
q^{*}
and some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
with
q\sim\varepsilon q^{*}
.

It remains to show that
K
is a normal subgroup of
C_{G}(t)
. Suppose that
\widetilde{K}
is a
2
-component of
C_{G}(t)
such that
K\cong\widetilde{K}
. Set
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}:=\mathcal{F}_{T\cap\widetilde{K}}(\widetilde{K})
. Since
\widetilde{K}
is subnormal in
C_{G}(t)
, it easily follows from
[
11
, Part I, Proposition 6.2]
that
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
is subnormal in
C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)
. Moreover,
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\cong\mathcal{C}_{1}
as
\widetilde{K}\cong K
. Hence
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
is a component of
C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)
. But as a consequence of Lemma
5.3
, there is no component of
C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle t\rangle)
which is isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and different from
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. So we have
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
. The uniqueness in the first statement of the proposition implies that
K=\widetilde{K}
. Consequently,
C_{G}(t)
has no
2
-component which is different from
K
and isomorphic to
K
. So
K
is characteristic and hence normal in
C_{G}(t)
.
∎

From now on,
K
,
q^{*}
and
\varepsilon
will always have the meanings given to them by Proposition
6.1
.

Our next goal is to prove the existence and uniqueness of a normal subgroup
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}:=C_{G}(t)/O(C_{G}(t))
such that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}\cong SL_{2}(q^{*})
, and to show that the image
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
of
K
in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
are the only subgroups of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
which are components or solvable
2
-components of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
. First, we need to prove some lemmas.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
A\in W
and
B\in V
such that
\mathrm{det}(A)\mathrm{det}(B)=1
. Let

m:=\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}Z(SL_{n}(q))\in T.

Set
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}:=C_{G}(t)/O(C_{G}(t))
. Then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}
centralizes
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
if and only if
A\in Z(GL_{n-2}(q))
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.5
, we have
m\in Z(\mathcal{C}_{1}\langle m\rangle)
if and only if
A\in Z(GL_{n-2}(q))
. Let
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
be the subsystem of
\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C})
corresponding to
\mathcal{C}_{1}
under the isomorphism from
\mathcal{F}_{T}(C_{G}(t))
to
\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C})
given by Corollary
2.12
. Then we have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\in Z(\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\rangle)
if and only if
A\in Z(GL_{n-2}(q))
. So it is enough to show that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\in Z(\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\rangle)
if and only if
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}
centralizes
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
. It is easy to note that
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}=\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
. As a consequence of Proposition
6.1
, we have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}\trianglelefteq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
. By
[
34
, Proposition 1]
, we have

\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\rangle=\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\rangle}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\rangle).

Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}
is a
2
-element of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
, we have
O(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\rangle)=O(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})=1
. Applying
[
23
, Corollary 1]
, it follows that the center of the product
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\rangle
is equal to the center of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\rangle
. It follows that that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\in Z(\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}\rangle)
if and only if
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{m}
centralizes
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
, as required.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose that
q^{*}=3
. Let
C:=C_{G}(t)
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}:=C/O(C)
. Then:

(i)

The factor group
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}/\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
is a
2
-group.

(ii)

The centralizer
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
is core-free.

(iii)

The factor group
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})/C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
is core-free.

Proof.

Clearly,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}/\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
is isomorphic to a subgroup of
\mathrm{Out}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
. Since
q^{*}=3
, we have that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}\cong SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(3)
. By Propositions
3.40
and
3.42
,
\mathrm{Out}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
is a
2
-group. So (i) holds.

Set
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{0}:=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
. As a consequence of (i),
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})/C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{0}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
is a
2
-group. Hence, in order to prove (ii), it suffices to show that
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{0}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
is core-free. As
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
, we have
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{0}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})=C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
. It follows that
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{0}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})/C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})\cong C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})/(C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})\cap C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}))=C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})/Z(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
. By Corollary
3.8
,
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
is core-free. This easily implies that
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})/Z(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
is core-free. It follows that
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{0}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})/C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
is core-free. Consequently,
O(C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{0}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}))=O(C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}))=1
. So (ii) follows.

Finally, (iii) is true since
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})/C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{0}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
is a
2
-group and
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{0}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})/C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
is core-free.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}:=C_{G}(t)/O(C_{G}(t))
. Then there is a unique pair
({A_{1}}^{+},{A_{2}}^{+})
of normal subgroups
{A_{1}}^{+}
,
{A_{2}}^{+}
of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})^{\prime}
such that
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})^{\prime}={A_{1}}^{+}\times{A_{2}}^{+}
,
{A_{1}}^{+}\cong SL_{2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
,
{A_{2}}^{+}\cong SL_{n-4}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in{A_{1}}^{+}
. Moreover, the following hold.

(i)

{A_{1}}^{+}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
.

(ii)

{A_{2}}^{+}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
.

(iii)

There is a group isomorphism
\varphi:\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}\rightarrow SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
under which
{A_{1}}^{+}
corresponds to the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&I_{n-4}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in SL_{2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})\right\}

in
SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
and under which
{A_{2}}^{+}
corresponds to the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}I_{2}&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}\ :\ B\in SL_{n-4}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})\right\}

in
SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
.

Proof.

For each subsystem
\mathcal{G}
of
\mathcal{F}_{T}(C_{G}(t))
, we use
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
to denote the subsystem of
\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C})
corresponding to
\mathcal{G}
under the isomorphism from
\mathcal{F}_{T}(C_{G}(t))
to
\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C})
given by Corollary
2.12
. Note that
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}=\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
.

Set
H:=SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
. For each even natural number
i
with
2\leq i\leq n-2
, let
h_{i}
be the image of
\widetilde{h_{i}}:=\mathrm{diag}(-1,\dots,-1,1,\dots,1)\in SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
in
H
, where
-1
occurs precisely
i
times as a diagonal entry.

We claim that there is a group isomorphism
\varphi:\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}\rightarrow H
such that
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}}^{\varphi}=h_{i}
for some even
2\leq i<n-2
. By Proposition
6.1
, we have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}\cong K/O(K)\cong H
. As a consequence of Lemmas
3.3
and
3.4
, any involution of
SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
is conjugate to
\widetilde{h_{i}}
for some even
2\leq i\leq n-2
. Since any involution of
H
is induced by an involution of
SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
, it follows that any involution of
H
is conjugate to
h_{i}
for some even
2\leq i\leq n-2
. As
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}
is an involution of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
, it follows that there is an isomorphism
\varphi:\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}\rightarrow H
mapping
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}
to
h_{i}
for some even
2\leq i\leq n-2
. Assume that
i=n-2
. Then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}
is central in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
. Thus
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in Z(\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}})
and hence
u\in Z(\mathcal{C}_{1})
. This is a contradiction to Lemma
3.18
and the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. So we have
i<n-2
.

Set
h:={\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}}^{\varphi}=h_{i}
. Also, let
H_{1}
be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&I_{n-2-i}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in SL_{i}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})\right\}

in
H
, and let
H_{2}
be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}I_{i}&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}\ :\ B\in SL_{n-2-i}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})\right\}

in
H
. For
j\in\{1,2\}
, let
{A_{j}}^{+}
be the subgroup of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
corresponding to
H_{j}
under
\varphi
.

We now proceed in a number of steps in order to complete the proof.

(1)
We have
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})^{\prime}={A_{1}}^{+}{A_{2}}^{+}
,
[{A_{1}}^{+},{A_{2}}^{+}]=1
,
{A_{1}}^{+},{A_{2}}^{+}\trianglelefteq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in{A_{1}}^{+}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\not\in{A_{2}}^{+}
.

It is easy to note that
C_{H}(h)^{\prime}
is the central product of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
and that
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are normal in
C_{H}(h)
. Therefore,
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})^{\prime}
is the central product of
{A_{1}}^{+}
and
{A_{2}}^{+}
, and
{A_{1}}^{+},{A_{2}}^{+}
are normal in
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
. By definition of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, we have
h\in H_{1}
and
h\not\in H_{2}
. Thus
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in{A_{1}}^{+}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\not\in{A_{2}}^{+}
.

(2)
We have
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}))
, and
\{\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}}({A_{1}}^{+}),\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}}({A_{2}}^{+})\}
contains every component of
C_{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}(\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle)
.

By Lemma
5.8
(i), we have that
\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle
is fully
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
-centralized. So we have
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}))
.

Set
P:=C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})^{\varphi}\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(C_{H}(h))
. It is easy to note that the
2
-components of
C_{H}(h)
are precisely the quasisimple elements of
\{H_{1},H_{2}\}
. Proposition
2.16
implies that the components of
\mathcal{F}_{P}(C_{H}(h))
are precisely the quasisimple elements of
\{\mathcal{F}_{P\cap H_{1}}(H_{1}),\mathcal{F}_{P\cap H_{2}}(H_{2})\}
.

Thus the components of
C_{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}(\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle)=\mathcal{F}_{C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})}(C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}))
are precisely the quasisimple elements of
\{\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}}({A_{1}}^{+}),\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}}({A_{2}}^{+})\}
.

(3)
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}
are subgroups of
\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}(\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle))
and are strongly closed in
C_{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}(\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle)
.

We have
\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}(\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle))=C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})\cap C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})^{\prime}
by the focal subgroup theorem
[
24
, Chapter 7, Theorem 3.4]
. So the claim follows from (1).

(4)
Suppose that
n=6
and
q\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
. Then we have
i=2
and hence
{A_{1}}^{+}\cong SL_{2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})\cong{A_{2}}^{+}
. Moreover,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
.

Since
n=6
and
2\leq i<n-2=4
, we have
i=2
. Thus
{A_{1}}^{+}\cong H_{1}\cong SL_{2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})\cong H_{2}\cong{A_{2}}^{+}
. By Proposition
6.1
, we have
q\sim\varepsilon q^{*}
, whence
q^{*}\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
. Clearly,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}({A_{1}}^{+})
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}({A_{2}}^{+})
. Lemma
3.12
implies that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}\cong Q_{8}\cong\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}
. By Lemma
5.8
(iii),
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
are the only subgroups of
\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}(\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle))
which are isomorphic to
Q_{8}
and strongly closed in
C_{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}(\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle)
. So, by (3),
\{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}\}=\{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}\}
. We have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
, and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\not\in{A_{2}}^{+}
by (1). It follows that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
.

(5)
Suppose that
n=6
and
q\equiv 1
or
7\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
, or that
n\geq 7
. Then we have
i=2
, and hence
{A_{1}}^{+}\cong SL_{2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
and
{A_{2}}^{+}\cong SL_{n-4}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
. Moreover,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
.

We begin by proving that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
. As a consequence of Lemma
5.8
(v),
C_{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}(\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle)
has a component with Sylow group
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
. Applying (2), we may conclude that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}
or
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}
. Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in A_{1}^{+}
by (1), but
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\not\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
, we have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
.

We show next that
i=2
. Using Proposition
3.19
, or using the order formulas for
|SL_{n-4}(q^{*})|
and
|SU_{n-4}(q^{*})|
given by
[
33
, Proposition 1.1 and Corollary 11.29]
, we see that

|SL_{n-4}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})|_{2}=|SL_{n-4}(q)|_{2}=|T_{2}|=|{A_{2}}^{+}|_{2}=|H_{2}|_{2}=|SL_{n-2-i}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})|_{2}.

Using the order formulas cited above, we may conclude that
n-2-i=n-4
, whence
i=2
. In particular,
{A_{1}}^{+}\cong SL_{2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
and
{A_{2}}^{+}\cong SL_{n-4}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
.

It remains to prove
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
. If
q\equiv 1
or
7\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
, then Lemma
5.8
(v) shows that
C_{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}(\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle)
has a component with Sylow group
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
. Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
, but
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\not\in{A_{2}}^{+}
, we have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
by (2).

Now suppose that
q\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
. Then we have
q^{*}\equiv 3
or
5\ \mathrm{mod}\ 8
since
q\sim\varepsilon q^{*}
. So, by Lemma
3.12
, a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
{A_{1}}^{+}
is isomorphic to
Q_{8}
. In particular,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}\cong Q_{8}
. By (3),
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}
is a subgroup of
\mathfrak{foc}(C_{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}(\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle))
and is strongly closed in
C_{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}}(\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle)
. Moreover, by (1),
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}
centralizes
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}
. Lemma
5.8
(iv) now implies that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\cap{A_{1}}^{+}
.

(6)
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})^{\prime}={A_{1}}^{+}\times{A_{2}}^{+}
.

We have
{A_{1}}^{+}\cong SL_{2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
by (4) and (5), and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in Z({A_{1}}^{+})
by (1). It follows that
Z({A_{1}}^{+})=\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\rangle
. By (1),
{A_{1}}^{+}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}\leq Z({A_{1}}^{+})
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\not\in{A_{1}}^{+}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}
. It follows that
{A_{1}}^{+}\cap{A_{2}}^{+}=1
. So (1) implies that
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})^{\prime}={A_{1}}^{+}\times{A_{2}}^{+}
.

(7)
Assume that
{A_{1}}^{\circ}
and
{A_{2}}^{\circ}
are normal subgroups of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})^{\prime}
such that
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})^{\prime}={A_{1}}^{\circ}\times{A_{2}}^{\circ}
,
{A_{1}}^{\circ}\cong SL_{2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
,
{A_{2}}^{\circ}\cong SL_{n-4}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in{A_{1}}^{\circ}
. Then
{A_{1}}^{\circ}={A_{1}}^{+}
and
{A_{2}}^{\circ}={A_{2}}^{+}
.

Let
j\in\{1,2\}
. As a consequence of (4) and (5),
{A_{j}}^{+}
is either quasisimple or isomorphic to
SL_{2}(3)
. In either case, it is easy to see that
{A_{j}}^{+}
is indecomposable, i.e.
{A_{j}}^{+}
cannot be written as an internal direct product of two proper normal subgroups. Moreover,
|{A_{1}}^{+}/({A_{1}}^{+})^{\prime}|
and
|Z({A_{2}}^{+})|
as well as
|{A_{2}}^{+}/({A_{2}}^{+})^{\prime}|
and
|Z({A_{1}}^{+})|
are coprime. A consequence of the Krull-Remak-Schmidt theorem, namely
[
35
, Kapitel I, Satz 12.6]
, implies that
\{{A_{1}}^{+},{A_{2}}^{+}\}=\{{A_{1}}^{\circ},{A_{2}}^{\circ}\}
. Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in{A_{1}}^{+}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\not\in{A_{2}}^{\circ}
, we have
{A_{1}}^{+}={A_{1}}^{\circ}
and
{A_{2}}^{+}={A_{2}}^{\circ}
.

(8)
The isomorphism
\varphi:\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}\rightarrow H
maps
{A_{1}}^{+}
to the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&I_{n-4}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in SL_{2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})\right\}

in
H
and
{A_{2}}^{+}
to the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}I_{2}&\\
&B\end{pmatrix}\ :\ B\in SL_{n-4}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})\right\}

in
H
.

By (4) and (5), we have
i=2
. So the claim follows from the definitions of
{A_{1}}^{+}
and
{A_{2}}^{+}
.
∎

From now on,
{A_{1}}^{+}
and
{A_{2}}^{+}
will always have the meanings given to them by Lemma
6.4
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
C:=C_{G}(t)
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}:=C/O(C)
. Then
{A_{1}}^{+}
and
{A_{2}}^{+}
are normal subgroups of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
.

Proof.

We have
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})\trianglelefteq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
as
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}\trianglelefteq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
. Thus
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})^{\prime}\trianglelefteq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
. Having this observed, the lemma is immediate from Lemma
6.4
.
∎

Let
C:=C_{G}(t)
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}:=C/O(C)
. Next we introduce certain preimages of
{A_{1}}^{+}
and
{A_{2}}^{+}
in
C_{C}(u)
. By Corollary
2.2
, we have
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
. We may see from Proposition
2.4
that there is a bijection from the set of
2
-components of
C_{C}(u)
to the set of
2
-components of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
sending each
2
-component
A
of
C_{C}(u)
to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{A}
.

Suppose that
q^{*}\neq 3
. Then
{A_{1}}^{+}
is a component and hence a
2
-component of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
. We use
A_{1}
to denote the
2
-component of
C_{C}(u)
corresponding to
{A_{1}}^{+}
under the bijection described above.

Suppose that
q^{*}\neq 3
or
n\geq 7
. Then
{A_{2}}^{+}
is a component and hence a
2
-component of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
. We use
A_{2}
to denote the
2
-component of
C_{C}(u)
corresponding to
{A_{2}}^{+}
under the bijection described above.

Suppose that
q^{*}=3
. By Lemma
6.3
(ii),
O(C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}))=1
. So the factor group
C_{C}(u)/(C_{C}(u)\cap O(C))
is core-free, whence
O(C_{C}(u))=C_{C}(u)\cap O(C)
. Let
O(C_{C}(u))\leq A_{1}\leq C_{C}(u)
such that
A_{1}/O(C_{C}(u))
corresponds to
{A_{1}}^{+}
under the natural group isomorphism
C_{C}(u)/O(C_{C}(u))\rightarrow C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
. Furthermore, if
n=6
, let
O(C_{C}(u))\leq A_{2}\leq C_{C}(u)
such that
A_{2}/O(C_{C}(u))
corresponds to
{A_{2}}^{+}
under the natural group isomorphism
C_{C}(u)/O(C_{C}(u))\rightarrow C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
.

Lemma 6.6
.

We have
T_{1}\leq A_{1}
and
T_{2}\leq A_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
i\in\{1,2\}
. Set
C:=C_{G}(t)
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}:=C/O(C)
.

Let
C_{C}(u)\cap O(C)\leq\widetilde{A_{i}}\leq C_{C}(u)
such that
\widetilde{A_{i}}/(C_{C}(u)\cap O(C))
corresponds to
{A_{i}}^{+}
under the natural group isomorphism
C_{C}(u)/(C_{C}(u)\cap O(C))\rightarrow C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
. We have
T_{i}\leq C_{C}(u)
and, by Lemma
6.4
,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}\leq{A_{i}}^{+}
. Thus
T_{i}\leq\widetilde{A_{i}}
. If
{A_{i}}^{+}\cong SL_{2}(3)
, then we have
A_{i}=\widetilde{A_{i}}
and thus
T_{i}\leq A_{i}
. Assume now that
{A_{i}}^{+}
is a component of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
. Then
A_{i}
is the
2
-component of
C_{C}(u)
associated to the
2
-component
\widetilde{A_{i}}/(C_{C}(u)\cap O(C))
of
C_{C}(u)/(C_{C}(u)\cap O(C))
. So, by Proposition
2.4
,
A_{i}=O^{2^{\prime}}(\widetilde{A_{i}})
, and hence
T_{i}\leq A_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

There is an element
g\in G
such that
{T_{1}}^{g}=X_{2}
and
{X_{2}}^{g}=T_{1}
. For each such
g\in G
, we have
u^{g}=t
and
t^{g}=u
.

Proof.

The first statement easily follows from
\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)=\mathcal{F}_{S}(PSL_{n}(q))
. By Lemma
3.12
, the groups
T_{1}
and
X_{2}
are generalized quaternion. So
u
is the only involution of
T_{1}
and
t
is the only involution of
X_{2}
. Thus
u^{g}=t
and
t^{g}=u
for any
g\in G
with
{T_{1}}^{g}=X_{2}
and
{X_{2}}^{g}=T_{1}
.
∎

With the above lemmas at hand, we can now prove the following proposition.

Proposition 6.8
.

Take an element
g\in G
such that
{T_{1}}^{g}=X_{2}
and
{X_{2}}^{g}=T_{1}
. Set
C:=C_{G}(t)
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}:=C/O(C)
. Let
L:={A_{1}}^{g}
. Then the following hold.

(i)

L\leq C_{C}(u)
.

(ii)

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
is subnormal in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}\cong SL_{2}(q^{*})
.

(iii)

The subgroups
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
are the only subgroups of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
which are components or solvable
2
-components of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
. In particular,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
are normal subgroups of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.7
, we have
t^{g}=u
and
u^{g}=t
. Hence
C_{C}(u)^{g}=C_{C}(u)
. As
A_{1}
is a subgroup of
C_{C}(u)
, we thus have
L={A_{1}}^{g}\leq C_{C}(u)
. So (i) holds.

Before proving (ii), we show that
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
is a normal subgroup of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
containing
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}
. Since
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})\trianglelefteq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
, we have
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}\cap C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})\trianglelefteq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
. Because of Lemma
6.6
, we have
X_{2}={T_{1}}^{g}\leq{A_{1}}^{g}=L
. Thus
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\leq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
. By the definition of
X_{2}
and by Lemma
6.2
, we have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\leq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
. Thus
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\leq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
.

Note that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}
is generalized quaternion by Lemma
3.12
and in particular nonabelian.

We now prove (ii) for the case
q^{*}\neq 3
. Then
A_{1}
is a
2
-component of
C_{C}(u)
. As
g
normalizes
C_{C}(u)
and
L={A_{1}}^{g}
, it follows that
L
is a
2
-component of
C_{C}(u)
. So
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
is a
2
-component of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
. Moreover, we have
A_{1}/O(A_{1})\cong SL_{2}(q^{*})
since
A_{1}/(A_{1}\cap O(C))\cong\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{A}={A_{1}}^{+}\cong SL_{2}(q^{*})
. Hence
L/O(L)
is isomorphic to
SL_{2}(q^{*})
. The group
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})O(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L})/O(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L})
is normal in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}/O(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L})
, and it is nonabelian since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\leq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
. As
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}/O(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L})
is quasisimple, it follows that
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})O(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L})=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
. So
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
has odd index in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
. Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
is a
2
-component of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
, we have
O^{2^{\prime}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L})=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
. It follows that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}=C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})\leq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
. Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
is subnormal in
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
and
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})\leq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
, we have that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
is subnormal in
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
. Hence
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
is subnormal in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
. As
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
is core-free, we have
O(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L})=1
. It follows that
O(L)=L\cap O(C)
and hence
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}\cong L/O(L)\cong SL_{2}(q^{*})
. So we have proved (ii) for the case
q^{*}\neq 3
.

Assume now that
q^{*}=3
. Then
O(C_{C}(u))=C_{C}(u)\cap O(C)
,
O(C_{C}(u))\leq A_{1}\leq C_{C}(u)
, and
A_{1}/O(C_{C}(u))
corresponds to
{A_{1}}^{+}\cong SL_{2}(3)
under the natural isomorphism
C_{C}(u)/O(C_{C}(u))\rightarrow C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
. By Lemma
6.5
,
{A_{1}}^{+}
is normal in
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
. Hence,
A_{1}/O(C_{C}(u))
is a normal subgroup of
C_{C}(u)/O(C_{C}(u))
isomorphic to
SL_{2}(3)
. Since
g
normalizes
C_{C}(u)
and
L={A_{1}}^{g}
, it follows that
O(C_{C}(u))\leq L
and that
L/O(C_{C}(u))
is a normal subgroup of
C_{C}(u)/O(C_{C}(u))
isomorphic to
SL_{2}(3)
. Since
L/O(C_{C}(u))
corresponds to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
under the natural isomorphism
C_{C}(u)/O(C_{C}(u))\rightarrow C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
, it follows that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
is a normal subgroup of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
isomorphic to
SL_{2}(3)
. Recall that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\leq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})\trianglelefteq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
. As
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
has order
24
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}
has order
8
,
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
either equals
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
or has index
3
in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
. However, if the latter holds, then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})/C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
is a normal subgroup of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})/C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
of order
3
, which is a contradiction to Lemma
6.3
(iii). Thus
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}=C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})\leq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
. As
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}\trianglelefteq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
and
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})\leq C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
, it follows that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
is normal in
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K})
and hence subnormal in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
. So we have proved (ii) for the case
q^{*}=3
.

We now prove (iii). Clearly,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}
. Also
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}
since
|\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}|=|SL_{2}(q)|_{2}=|SL_{2}(q^{*})|_{2}=|\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}|_{2}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\leq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
. As a consequence of Lemma
5.4
, the fusion system
\mathcal{F}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{T}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C})/(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X})
is nilpotent. Applying Lemma
2.17
, we may conclude that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
are the only subgroups of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
which are components or solvable
2
-components of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
. As
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{L}
are not isomorphic, both are characteristic and hence normal in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
.
∎

It is not difficult to observe that the definition of
L
in Proposition
6.8
is independent of the choice of
g
. From now on,
L
will always have the meaning given to it by the above proposition.

6.2.
2
-components of centralizers of involutions conjugate to
t_{i}
,
i\neq 2

Having described the components and the solvable
2
-components of the group
C_{G}(t)/O(C_{G}(t))
, we now turn our attention to centralizers of involutions of
G
not conjugate to
t
.

First we recall some notation from Section
5
. Let
1\leq i<n
. If
i
is even, then
t_{i}
denotes the image of

\begin{pmatrix}I_{n-i}&\\
&-I_{i}\end{pmatrix}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
. We use
\rho
to denote an element of
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*}
with order
(n,q-1)
, and if
\rho
is a square in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, then
\mu
denotes an element of
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*}
with
\mu^{2}=\rho
. If
n
is even,
\rho
is a square in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and
i
is odd, then
t_{i}
is defined to be the image of

\begin{pmatrix}\mu I_{n-i}&\\
&-\mu I_{i}\end{pmatrix}\in SL_{n}(q)

in
PSL_{n}(q)
. It is easy to note that
t_{i}
lies in
T
and hence in
S
whenever
t_{i}
is defined.

Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the set of all subgroups
E
of
PSL_{n}(q)
such that there is some elementary abelian
2
-subgroup
\widetilde{E}\leq SL_{n}(q)
with
E=\widetilde{E}Z(SL_{n}(q))/Z(SL_{n}(q))
. For each
3\leq i\leq n
, we define
\mathcal{S}_{i}
to be the set of all elements
E
of
\mathcal{S}
such that
E
contains a
PSL_{n}(q)
-conjugate of
t_{j}
for some even
2\leq j<i
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
1\leq i<n
such that
t_{i}
is defined. Assume that
i\neq 2
, and that
i\leq\frac{n}{2}
if
n
is even. Let
P
be a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t_{i})
and
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{P}(C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t_{i}))
. Then the following hold.

(i)

Assume that
i\not\in\{1,n-1\}
. Then
\mathcal{F}
has precisely two components. Denoting them in a suitable way by
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, the following hold.

(a)

\mathcal{E}_{1}
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{n-i}(q)
.

(b)

\mathcal{E}_{2}
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{i}(q)
.

(c)

Let
Y_{1}
be the Sylow group of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and let
Y_{2}
be the Sylow group of
\mathcal{E}_{2}
. Then
Y_{1}Y_{2}
is normal in
P
and
\mathcal{F}/Y_{1}Y_{2}
is nilpotent. The group
Y_{i}
, where
i\in\{1,2\}
, contains a
PSL_{n}(q)
-conjugate of
t
. Moreover, any elementary abelian subgroup of
Y_{1}
of rank at least
2
is contained in
\mathcal{S}_{n-i}
, and any elementary abelian subgroup of
Y_{2}
of rank at least
2
is contained in
\mathcal{S}_{i}
.

(ii)

Assume that
i=1
or
i=n-1
. Then
\mathcal{F}
has a unique component. This component is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{n-1}(q)
. If
Y
is its Sylow group, then
Y\trianglelefteq P
and
\mathcal{F}/Y
is nilpotent. Moreover, any elementary abelian subgroup of
Y
of rank at least
2
is contained in
\mathcal{S}_{n-1}
.

Proof.

Assume that
i\not\in\{1,n-1\}
. By hypothesis, we have
i\neq 2
, and
i\leq\frac{n}{2}
if
n
is even. It follows that
i\geq 3
and
n-i\geq 3
. Let
J_{1}
be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&I_{i}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in SL_{n-i}(q)\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
, and let
J_{2}
be the image of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}I_{n-i}&\\
&A\end{pmatrix}\ :\ A\in SL_{i}(q)\right\}

in
PSL_{n}(q)
. It is easy to note that
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
are the only
2
-components of
C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t_{i})
. Applying Proposition
2.16
and Lemma
3.21
, we may conclude that
\mathcal{E}_{1}:=\mathcal{F}_{P\cap J_{1}}(J_{1})
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}:=\mathcal{F}_{P\cap J_{2}}(J_{2})
are the only components of
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{P}(C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t_{i}))
. Clearly,
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{n-i}(q)
, while
\mathcal{E}_{2}
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{i}(q)
. Set
Y_{1}:=P\cap J_{1}
and
Y_{2}:=P\cap J_{2}
. It is easy to note that
Y_{1}Y_{2}=P\cap J_{1}J_{2}
. As
J_{1}J_{2}\trianglelefteq C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t_{i})
, it follows that
Y_{1}Y_{2}\trianglelefteq P
. By Lemma
2.11
,
\mathcal{F}/Y_{1}Y_{2}
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t_{i})/J_{1}J_{2}
, and it is easy to note that
C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(t_{i})/J_{1}J_{2}
is
2
-nilpotent. So
\mathcal{F}/Y_{1}Y_{2}
is nilpotent by
[
39
, Theorem 1.4]
. It is clear from the definitions of
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
that both
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
contain a
PSL_{n}(q)
-conjugate of
t
. Hence
Y_{k}
has an element which is
PSL_{n}(q)
-conjugate to
t
for
k\in\{1,2\}
. Clearly, any elementary abelian
2
-subgroup of
J_{k}
,
k\in\{1,2\}
, lies in
\mathcal{S}
. Moreover, any noncentral involution of
J_{1}
is
PSL_{n}(q)
-conjugate to
t_{j}
for some even
2\leq j<n-i
, and any noncentral involution of
J_{2}
is
PSL_{n}(q)
-conjugate to
t_{j}
for some even
2\leq j<i
. This implies that any elementary abelian subgroup of
Y_{1}
of rank at least
2
is contained in
\mathcal{S}_{n-i}
, and that any elementary abelian subgroup of
Y_{2}
of rank at least
2
is contained in
\mathcal{S}_{i}
. This completes the proof of (i).

We omit the proof of (ii) since it is very similar to the one of (i).
∎

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
1\leq i<n
such that
t_{i}
is defined. Assume that
i\not\in\{1,2,n-1\}
, and that
i\leq\frac{n}{2}
if
n
is even. Let
x
be an involution of
S
which is
G
-conjugate to
t_{i}
. Then
C_{G}(x)
has precisely two
2
-components. Denoting them in a suitable way by
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
, the following hold.

(i)

J_{1}/O(J_{1})
is isomorphic to
SL_{n-i}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{n-i}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
, where
\varepsilon
and
q^{*}
are as in Proposition
6.1
.

(ii)

J_{2}/O(J_{2})\cong SL_{i}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{i}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
, where
\varepsilon
and
q^{*}
are as in Proposition
6.1
.

(iii)

Any elementary abelian
2
-subgroup of
J_{1}
of rank at least
2
is
G
-conjugate to a subgroup of
S
lying in
\mathcal{S}_{n-i}
, and any elementary abelian
2
-subgroup of
J_{2}
of rank at least
2
is
G
-conjugate to a subgroup of
S
lying in
\mathcal{S}_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)=\mathcal{F}_{S}(PSL_{n}(q))
. It suffices to prove the proposition under the assumption that
\langle x\rangle
is fully
\mathcal{F}
-centralized, and we will assume that this is the case. So we have
C_{S}(x)\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(C_{G}(x))
and
C_{S}(x)\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(x))
. Also,
\mathcal{F}_{C_{S}(x)}(C_{G}(x))=C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle x\rangle)=\mathcal{F}_{C_{S}(x)}(C_{PSL_{n}(q)}(x))
.

Clearly,
x
is
PSL_{n}(q)
-conjugate to
t_{i}
. So Lemma
6.9
(i) shows together with Lemma
5.10
(i) that there exist two distinct
2
-components
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
of
C_{G}(x)
satisfying the following conditions, where
Y_{1}:=C_{S}(x)\cap J_{1}
and
Y_{2}:=C_{S}(x)\cap J_{2}
.

(1)

\mathcal{F}_{Y_{1}}(J_{1})
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{n-i}(q)
.

(2)

\mathcal{F}_{Y_{2}}(J_{2})
is isomorphic to the
2
-fusion system of
SL_{i}(q)
.

(3)

Y_{1}Y_{2}
is normal in
C_{S}(x)
, and
C_{\mathcal{F}}(\langle x\rangle)/Y_{1}Y_{2}
is nilpotent.

(4)

For
k\in\{1,2\}
,
Y_{k}
contains a
G
-conjugate of
t
.

(5)

Any elementary abelian abelian subgroup of
Y_{1}
of rank at least
2
lies in
\mathcal{S}_{n-i}
, and any elementary abelian subgroup of
Y_{2}
of rank at least
2
lies in
\mathcal{S}_{i}
.

By (3) and Corollary
2.18
,
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
are the only
2
-components of
C_{G}(x)
. It remains to show that
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
satisfy (i)-(iii). As
Y_{k}\in\mathrm{Syl}_{2}(J_{k})
for
k\in\{1,2\}
, (5) implies (iii).

We now prove (ii). The proof of (i) will be omitted since it is very similar to the proof of (ii).

Let
s
be an element of
J_{1}
which is
G
-conjugate to
t
. Set
C:=C_{G}(s)
,
\widehat{C}:=C/O(C)
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}:=C_{G}(x)/O(C_{G}(x))
.

Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J}
are distinct components of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}
, we have
[\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J}]=1
by
[
37
, 6.5.3]
. As
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{s}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J}
, it follows that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J}
is a component of
C_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{s})
. As a consequence of Corollary
2.2
and Proposition
2.4
,
C_{G}(x)\cap C
has a
2
-component
H
with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J}
.

By assumption,
s
is
G
-conjugate to
t
. So, by Proposition
6.8
,
\widehat{C}
has a unique normal subgroup
K^{+}
isomorphic to
SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{n-2}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
and a unique normal subgroup
L^{+}
isomorphic to
SL_{2}(q^{*})
. Moreover,
K^{+}
and
L^{+}
are the only subgroups of
\widehat{C}
which are components or solvable
2
-components of
\widehat{C}
.

Clearly,
\widehat{H}
is a
2
-component of
C_{\widehat{C}}(\widehat{x})
. Lemma
2.5
implies that
\widehat{H}
is a
2
-component of
C_{K^{+}}(\widehat{x})
or of
C_{L^{+}}(\widehat{x})
. By Corollary
3.46
(i), we even have that
\widehat{H}
is a component of
C_{K^{+}}(\widehat{x})
or
C_{L^{+}}(\widehat{x})
. It is easy to note that
\widehat{H}/Z(\widehat{H})\cong H/Z^{*}(H)\cong\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J}/Z(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J})
. By Corollary
3.46
(ii), we have
\widehat{H}/Z(\widehat{H})\not\cong M_{11}
, and so
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J}/Z(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J})\not\cong M_{11}
. Now (2) and Lemma
5.10
(ii) imply that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J}\cong SL_{i}^{\varepsilon_{0}}(q_{0})/O(SL_{i}^{\varepsilon_{0}}(q_{0}))
for some nontrivial odd prime power
q_{0}
and some
\varepsilon_{0}\in\{+,-\}
with
q\sim\varepsilon_{0}q_{0}
. Hence
\widehat{H}/Z(\widehat{H})\cong\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J}/Z(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{J})\cong PSL_{i}^{\varepsilon_{0}}(q_{0})
. Note that
\varepsilon q^{*}\sim q\sim\varepsilon_{0}q_{0}
and in particular
({q^{*}}^{2}-1)_{2}=({q_{0}}^{2}-1)_{2}
. Applying Corollary
3.46
(iii), we may conclude that
q_{0}=q^{*}
and
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon
. Consequently, we have
J_{2}/O(J_{2})\cong SL_{i}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{i}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
. So we have proved (ii).
∎

The proof of the following proposition runs along the same lines as that of the previous result.

Proposition 6.11
.

Suppose that
n
is odd and
i=n-1
, or that
n
is even,
i=1
and
t_{1}
is defined. Let
x
be an involution of
S
which is
G
-conjugate to
t_{i}
. Then
C_{G}(x)
has precisely one
2
-component
J
. We have
J/O(J)\cong SL_{n-1}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*})/O(SL_{n-1}^{\varepsilon}(q^{*}))
, where
\varepsilon
and
q^{*}
are as in Proposition
6.1
. Moreover, any elementary abelian
2
-subgroup of
J
of rank at least
2
is
G
-conjugate to a subgroup of
S
lying in
\mathcal{S}_{n-1}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{S}(G)=\mathcal{F}_{S}(PSL_{n}(q))
